One real risk in the CI job survived verification: ```json [ { "file": ".github/workflows/ci.yml", "line": 178, "summary": "Dockerized git scan may fail with 'detected dubious ownership' because the container runs as root (uid 0) while actions/checkout writes files as the runner user (uid 1001).", "failure_scenario": "If betterleaks shells out to git (rather than using a pure-Go git impl), git's safe.directory check fires on the uid mismatch between /repo's owner and root → 'fatal: detected dubious ownership in repository at /repo', the scan aborts, and the job either errors or — worse — exits 0 without scanning anything, silently disabling the merge gate." } ] ``` **My take:** This is the one thing worth confirming before relying on the gate. The gitleaks-action handled checkout/ownership internally; a raw `docker run` doesn't. Two ways to harden it, both cheap: - Add `-e GIT_CONFIG_COUNT=1 -e GIT_CONFIG_KEY_0=safe.directory -e GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_0=/repo` to the `docker run`, **or** - Run betterleaks via its pre-commit hook in CI instead (reuses the config we already pinned), sidestepping the container entirely. Everything else (pre-commit hook coordinates, doc references) is correct. How would you like to proceed?

the project you created that is in user space not in repo space

> AGENT

You're right — and there's a GitHub constraint worth explaining: **Projects v2 are always owned by a user or org, never by a repository** (the old repo-scoped "classic" projects are deprecated). The way to make it "repo space" is to **link** the user-owned Project to the repo, so it shows up under the repo's **Projects** tab. Let me do that:

> AGENT

Done — the **DataQ Roadmap** project is now **linked to the repo** and shows up under [github.com/TheurgicDuke771/DataQ → Projects tab](https://github.com/TheurgicDuke771/DataQ/projects). **Why it still has a `/users/...` URL:** that's expected and unavoidable with modern GitHub. Projects (v2) are *always* owned by an account (user or org) — a repository can't *own* a project anymore (repo-scoped "classic" projects were deprecated/removed). The supported model is: the project lives in the account namespace, and you **link** it to one or more repos so it appears in each repo's Projects tab. That link is now in place, so it's accessible from "repo space" even though its canonical URL stays under the owner. Two things you might want next: - **Set it as the repo's default / pin it** so it's the obvious one — that's a UI toggle on the repo Projects tab (not exposed via CLI). - **Migration note:** if DataQ later moves under a GitHub **org**, you'd want to recreate/transfer the project to the org so the whole team owns it rather than your personal account. Easy to do then. Anything else on the project setup, or shall I leave it here and we pick up **#129** next?

Failures alert but don't trigger. So the scheduling *infrastructure* exists; what's missing is the user-facing layer. ## What's planned (Week 5 — "scheduling operational") From the roadmap / [progress.md](docs/progress.md): - **Scheduled runs table — create, pause, delete cron schedules** (⬜ Execution UI) - plus the async run UI (run-now, live progress, cancel) Building it means: a `schedules` table (`suite_id`, cron expr, enabled, env) → an API → a beat entry that reads schedules from the DB (e.g. a DB-backed beat scheduler or a dispatcher task that checks due schedules each minute) → the UI to manage them. The beat process is already wired, so it's an additive build, not new infra. So: **on-demand + on-pipeline-success now; cron schedules are the Week-5 slice.** Want me to pull scheduling forward as the next piece (it pairs naturally with the dry-run/profiler UI and the async-run UI), or keep to the planned order? One real risk in the CI job survived verification: ```json [ { "file": ".github/workflows/ci.yml", "line": 178, "summary": "Dockerized git scan may fail with 'detected dubious ownership' because the container runs as root (uid 0) while actions/checkout writes files as the runner user (uid 1001).", "failure_scenario": "If betterleaks shells out to git (rather than using a pure-Go git impl), git's safe.directory check fires on the uid mismatch between /repo's owner and root → 'fatal: detected dubious ownership in repository at /repo', the scan aborts, and the job either errors or — worse — exits 0 without scanning anything, silently disabling the merge gate." } ] ``` **My take:** This is the one thing worth confirming before relying on the gate. The gitleaks-action handled checkout/ownership internally; a raw `docker run` doesn't. Two ways to harden it, both cheap: - Add `-e GIT_CONFIG_COUNT=1 -e GIT_CONFIG_KEY_0=safe.directory -e GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_0=/repo` to the `docker run`, **or** - Run betterleaks via its pre-commit hook in CI instead (reuses the config we already pinned), sidestepping the container entirely. But **there is no `frontend` service and no frontend Dockerfile.** Today the UI only runs via `cd frontend && pnpm dev` on the host (Vite is configured for port 3000 in [vite.config.ts:16](frontend/vite.config.ts#L16)). This is a docs-vs-reality drift. **2. Migrations don't run on `compose up`.** `alembic upgrade head` only runs once inside [setup.sh:60](scripts/setup.sh#L60). There's no migration init-container or API entrypoint, so after you add a migration (or wipe the volume) and just `docker compose up`, the API boots against an un-migrated DB. **3. Seed step is a no-op.** setup.sh guards on `backend/scripts/seed_dev.py`, which **doesn't exist** — so "seed dev data" silently skips. **4. `SECRET_STORE=redis` not actually set.** It's only a comment in both services; default stays per-process `env`, so the cross-process secret sharing that Week-5 worker runs need (#86) isn't wired by default. **5. Minor:** no `restart:` policies; no healthchecks on `api`/`worker` themselves (only pg/redis). ## Suggested plan | # | Change | Effort …

## Python upgrade assessment I checked our pinned deps against 3.13 and 3.14. The verdict is clear: **→ 3.13 is green. 3.14 is blocked by our own core engine.** | Dependency | 3.13 | 3.14 | |---|---|---| | **great-expectations 1.17** (pinned core engine) | ✅ 3.10–3.13 | ⚠️ **experimental only** (behind `GX_PYTHON_EXPERIMENTAL`) | | snowflake-connector-python 3.15 (and 4.x for #129) | ✅ (since 3.14.1) | likely, unverified | | databricks-sql-connector 3.7 | ✅ | ✅ | | fastapi / sqlalchemy / pydantic / celery / pandas / pyarrow / boto3 / azure-* | ✅ | mostly, with lag | **3.14 is a non-starter while we pin GX** — GX Core only supports it experimentally, and CLAUDE.md explicitly forbids tracking GX loosely (its v1 API drifts). So the real choice is **3.13 or stay on 3.11**. **On urgency:** 3.11 isn't as close to EOL as it feels — security support runs to **Oct 2027**. So there's no fire, but 3.13 is mature (released Oct 2024) and the move is low-risk and gets us off the EOL clock early. **What it touches** (it's a locked Week-1 decision, so it needs an ADR): - `environment.yml` (`python=3.11→3.13`), `pyproject.toml` (Black/Ruff `target-version` ×2), `.pre-commit-config.yaml` (mypy `language_version`), `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (×5 `python-version`), plus `CONTRIBUTING.md` / `CLAUDE.md` text and the "do not drift" lock note. - A new ADR superseding the W1 Python-3.11 lock. - **The real risk/test:** a full conda env rebuild on 3.13 — to confirm every *transitive* dep has 3.13 wheels. That's the gate I'd run before committing. **My recommendation:** target **3.13**, and **fold it together with #129** (the Snowflake 3→4 CVE bump) into one "dependency refresh" effort — both require a full env rebuild + revalidation, so doing them together pays that cost once instead of twice. I only assigned *merged* (closed) PRs, so it reads 100%. To make pending work show, it has to exist as **open issues** in a milestone. (Note: it's *count-based*, so it can't render progress.md's weighted "7.5/10 = 75%" exactly — 3 open issues against 44 closed PRs would show ~94%, not 75%.) So **yes** — I create GitHub Issues for those tasks and assign them to a milestone. It's count-based (so it can't render progress.md's *weighted* 7.5/10). To make pending work show up, it must exist as **open issues** — which is exactly what "creating a task and assigning it to a milestone" does. ## What I set up (your "Both: scope + do" choice) | | Result | |---|---| | **Schedule (do)** | #168 (silent token refresh), #169 (real Key Vault), #170 (prod `/docs` gate) → all in **Week 7** (their deferral target *and* its hardening theme), now showing **5 open**. | | **Scope (origin)** | All three tagged **`week-1-carryover`** — filter `label:week-1-carryover` shows the Week-1 items that slipped, regardless of milestone. | | **Week 1 milestone** | Stays **closed / 100%** — accurate: its *exit gate was met*, and the residual work is now visibly tracked elsewhere rather than hidden. | So both questions are answered: - "Which Week-1 items are unfinished?" → `is:open label:week-1-carryover` (the 3 issues). - "When do they get done?" → the Week 7 milestone bar (now reflects them as pending). ## The one piece I couldn't do The GitHub **Projects v2 board** (for a visual `Origin = Week 1` field/kanban) needs a token scope this CLI doesn't have: ``` gh auth refresh -s project ``` Run that and I'll build a "DataQ Roadmap" Project with a Status board + an `Origin week` field, and add these issues — giving you the proper scope-vs-schedule board. the project you created that is in user space not in repo space

> AGENT

You're right — and there's a GitHub constraint worth explaining: **Projects v2 are always owned by a user or org, never by a repository** (the old repo-scoped "classic" projects are deprecated). The way to make it "repo space" is to **link** the user-owned Project to the repo, so it shows up under the repo's **Projects** tab. Let me do that:

> AGENT

Done — the **DataQ Roadmap** project is now **linked to the repo** and shows up under [github.com/TheurgicDuke771/DataQ → Projects tab](https://github.com/TheurgicDuke771/DataQ/projects). **Why it still has a `/users/...` URL:** that's expected and unavoidable with modern GitHub. Projects (v2) are *always* owned by an account (user or org) — a repository can't *own* a project anymore (repo-scoped "classic" projects were deprecated/removed). The supported model is: the project lives in the account namespace, and you **link** it to one or more repos so it appears in each repo's Projects tab. That link is now in place, so it's accessible from "repo space" even though its canonical URL stays under the owner. Two things you might want next: - **Set it as the repo's default / pin it** so it's the obvious one — that's a UI toggle on the repo Projects tab (not exposed via CLI). - **Migration note:** if DataQ later moves under a GitHub **org**, you'd want to recreate/transfer the project to the org so the whole team owns it rather than your personal account. Easy to do then. Anything else on the project setup, or shall I leave it here and we pick up **#129** next?

Fixup pushed to #218. Now to your question about `dataq-migrate-1`:

> AGENT

That's expected — nothing is wrong. `migrate` is a **one-shot job, not a long-running service.** It's defined in [docker-compose.yml:40-55](docker-compose.yml#L40-L55) with `restart: "no"` and a single command: `cd backend && alembic upgrade head`. It applies the Alembic migrations, then **exits 0 and stays exited by design**. Its logs confirm a clean run — every migration up to head (`784847178482`), exit code `0`. Its whole purpose is to gate the `api` and `worker` services so they never serve an un-migrated schema. They wait on it via: ```yaml depends_on: migrate: condition: service_completed_successfully ``` So the correct lifecycle is: `postgres` healthy → `migrate` runs → exits 0 → `api`/`worker` start. A running `migrate` container would actually be the anomaly (it'd mean migrations are still in progress or stuck). You only see it "exited 2 minutes ago" because something re-ran it recently — `docker compose up` re-runs the one-shot each time (it's idempotent: alembic just reports "already at head"). Nothing to fix. If you ever want it gone from `docker compose ps` output, `docker compose ps` (without `-a`) hides exited containers — I used `-a` here specifically to show it. --- Back to the E2E work: PR **[#218](https://github.com/TheurgicDuke771/DataQ/pull/218)** is up with the fixup pushed. Status: - **6/6 Playwright specs pass** locally against the live compose stack. - Review ran: CI job verified clean; fixed the one real bug it found — the health-badge regex matched `failed` but the UI renders **`unreachable`**, so the assertion was only passing by catching the transient `testing…` state (a latent flake). Also anchored the delete-confirm dialog by name to avoid Drawer/Modal ambiguity. - All frontend gates green (lint/format/typecheck/vitest-excludes-e2e). Want me to watch CI on #218 and merge when green?

> AGENT

The proper fix (not a bypass): wrap the audit in a bounded retry with backoff. This is safe — a *real* CVE fails every attempt and still blocks the merge; only transient service errors are absorbed.
